The **People's Mojahedin Organization of Iran** (**PMOI**), also known as **Mojahedin-e-Khalq** (**MEK**) or **Mojahedin-e-Khalq Organization** (**MKO**; [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language): سازمان مجاهدین خلق ایران, [romanised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Persian): *Sâzmân-e Mojâhedin-e Khalgh-e Irân*),[^cite_note-16] is an Iranian dissident organisation. It was an armed group until 2003, afterwards switching focus into a political group.[^cite_note-17] Its [headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Ashraf_3) is currently in [Albania](738.md). The group's ideology has been historically influenced by [Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Philosophy) and [revolutionary socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_socialism) philosophies, particularly through a re-interpretative approach to [Shia Islam](26961.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman200199-18][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian19891–2,_92-19][^cite_note-20] After the [Iranian Revolution](347268.md), the MEK opposed the new theocratic [Government of Iran](50913538.md), seeking to replace it with its own government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman20012-21][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian19891–2-22][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen200923-23] At one point the MEK was Iran's "largest and most active armed dissident group",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECimment2011276,_859-24] and it is still sometimes presented by Western political backers as a major [Iranian opposition](80207658.md) group.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman200197-25][^cite_note-26][^cite_note-27] The MEK is unpopular today within Iran, largely due to its siding with Iraq in the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War) and continued ties with the government of [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) afterwards.[^cite_note-popularity-28]

The MEK was founded in 1965 by leftist Iranian students affiliated with the [Freedom Movement of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Movement_of_Iran) to oppose the Shah [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md).[^cite_note-Iranian_Politics-4][^cite_note-auto10-29] The organisation contributed to overthrowing the Shah during the 1979 Iranian Revolution. It subsequently pursued the establishment of a democracy in Iran, particularly gaining support from Iran's middle class [intelligentsia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligentsia).[^cite_note-auto7-30][^cite_note-auto8-31][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001100-32] The MEK boycotted the [1979 constitutional referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/December_1979_Iranian_constitutional_referendum), which led to Khomeini barring MEK leader [Massoud Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massoud_Rajavi) from the [1980 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Iranian_presidential_election).[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-Katz=boycott-35][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson20092-36] The MEK organised the [20 June 1981 Iranian protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20_June_1981_Iranian_protests) against Khomeini and the ousting of President [Abolhassan Banisadr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolhassan_Banisadr). The protests were violently suppressed by the [Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps](1285472.md), which shot into the crowds, killing 50, before executing 23 further protesters who had been arrested, including teenage girls.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian198967-68,_206-207,219-37][^cite_note-merat2018-38][^cite_note-Katz=boycott-35] On 28 June, the MEK was implicated in the blowing up of the headquarters of the [Islamic Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republican_Party) (IRP) in the [Haft-e Tir bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haft-e_Tir_bombing), killing 74 officials and party members.[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-Petro-40][^cite_note-source2-41][^cite_note-ABC-CLIO-42][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson200957-43] A wave of killings and executions led by Khomeini's government followed, part of the [1981–1982 Iran massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981%E2%80%931982_Iran_massacres).[^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45][^cite_note-\:4-46]

Rajavi fled to Paris[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson200958-47][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989219-48][^cite_note-49] and during the exile, the underground network that remained in Iran continued to plan and carry out attacks.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989220-221,258-50][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson200985-51] It allegedly conducted the [August 1981 bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_Iranian_Prime_Minister's_office_bombing) that killed Iran's president and prime minister.[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001101-53][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson200985-51] In 1983, the MEK began meeting with Iraqi officials.[^cite_note-54][^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57] In 1986, France expelled the MEK at the request of Iran,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiazza19949–43-58][^cite_note-auto23-59] forcing it to relocate to [Camp Ashraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Ashraf) in Iraq. In 1987, it founded the "National Liberation Army of Iran" (NLA), with the sole objective of "toppling the Islamic Republic through military force from outside the country".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2018-60][^cite_note-61][^cite_note-62] During the Iran–Iraq War, the MEK then sided with Iraq, taking part in [Operation Forty Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Forty_Stars),[^cite_note-\:0-63][^cite_note-\:1-64][^cite_note-\:2-65][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2018-60] and [Operation Mersad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Mersad).[^cite_note-Saeed_Kamali-66][^cite_note-Farrokh_03-67] Following Operation Mersad, Iranian officials ordered the [mass execution of prisoners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_executions_of_Iranian_political_prisoners) said to support the MEK.[^cite_note-telegraph.co.uk-68][^cite_note-\:4-46] The group gained significant publicity in 2002 by announcing the existence of [Iranian nuclear facilities](721807.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001105-69][^cite_note-70] In 2003, the MEK's military wing signed a ceasefire agreement with the U.S. and was disarmed at Camp Ashraf.[^cite_note-disarmament-71]

Between 1997 and 2013, the MEK was on the lists of terrorist organisations of the US, Canada, EU, UK and Japan for various periods.[^cite_note-72] The MEK is designated as a [terrorist organisation](63578.md) by Iran.[^cite_note-bdt45cgf112-73] Critics have described the group as exhibiting traits of a "personality cult",[^cite_note-74] while its backers describe the group as proponents of "a free and democratic Iran" that could become the next government there.[^cite_note-75]

## History

### Early years (1965–1970)

Mohammad Hanifnejad

Ali-Asghar Badizadegan

Saeed Mohsen

Hanifnejad (left) and Badizadegan (middle) and Mohsen (right), the founders of the organisation

The Mojahedin-e-Khalq (MEK) was founded in 1965 by a group of [Tehran University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_University) students who had opposed the Shah [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md) in the 1950s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman200198-76][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982489-77][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson20092-36] They considered the mainstream [Liberation Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Movement_of_Iran) too moderate and ineffective,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982489-77] and aimed to establish a socialist state in Iran based on a modern and revolutionary interpretation of Islam that originated from Islamic texts like [Nahj al-Balagha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nahj_al-Balagha) and some of [Ali Shariati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Shariati)'s works.[^cite_note-78][^cite_note-Iranian_Politics-4][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClark201666-79][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian19891–2-22][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian198981–126-80][^cite_note-ReferenceA-81] MEK founders included [Mohammad Hanifnejad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Hanifnejad), [Saeed Mohsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saeed_Mohsen), and [Ali Asghar Badiazadegan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Asghar_Badiazadegan),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian198987-82] and it attracted primarily young, well-educated Iranians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989227–230-83] While MEK publications were banned in Iran, in its first five years, the group primarily engaged in ideological work.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian198988-84]

### Schism (1970–1978)

See also: [Organization of Struggle for the Emancipation of the Working Class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Struggle_for_the_Emancipation_of_the_Working_Class)

| 1971 | 1972 | 1973 | 1974 | 1975 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [Bahram Aram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahram_Aram) |  |  |  |  |
| Reza Rezaei<sup>a</sup> | Taghi Shahram |  |  |  |
| Kazem Zolanvar<sup>b</sup> | [Majid Sharif Vaghefi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majid_Sharif_Vaghefi)<sup>c</sup> |  |  |  |
| <sup>a</sup> Killed in action by [SAVAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAVAK) in 1973 <sup>b</sup> Arrested in 1972, executed in 1975 <sup>c</sup> Killed by Marxist offshoot in 1975 purge |  |  |  |  |

By August 1971, the MEK's Central Committee included Reza Rezai, Kazem Zolanvar, and Brahram Aram.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVahabzadeh2010168-85] Arrests and executions carried out by the Shah [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md)'s security services between 1971 and 1972, compounded by internal factional infighting, shattered the organisation.[^cite_note-thedailybeast.com-86]

During the 1970s, the MEK opposed the Shah and [SAVAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAVAK), whom they described as "anti-patriotic, anti-Islamic, and collaborative with Imperialist and anti-popular oppressors".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiazza199414-87][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson20092-36][^cite_note-Abedin-88]

Between August and September 1971, SAVAK arrested and executed many MEK members including its co-founders.[^cite_note-Ḥaqšenās-89] Some surviving members restructured the group by replacing the central cadre with a three-man [central committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_committee). Each of the three central committee members led a separate branch of the organisation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989136-90] Two of the original central committee members were replaced in 1972 and 1973, and the replacing members were in charge of leading the organisation until the internal purge of 1975.[^cite_note-Ḥaqšenās-89]

By 1973, MEK members that declared themselves Marxist–Leninist launched an "internal ideological struggle",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVahabzadeh2010167–169-91] and by 1975 two opposing MEK factions had formed, one being Muslim and the other Marxist.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989145-92] The Marxist offshoot asserted that "they had reached the conclusion that Marxism, not Islam, was the true revolutionary philosophy".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982493-93] Members who did not convert to Marxism were expelled or reported to SAVAK.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVahabzadeh2010167–169-91] This led to two rival Mojahedin, each with its own publication, its own organisation, and its own activities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982493–4-94] The Marxist offshoot was initially known as the Mojahedin M.L. (Marxist–Leninist). A few months before the Iranian Revolution, the majority of the Marxist Mojahedin renamed themselves [Peykar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Struggle_for_the_Emancipation_of_the_Working_Class) (Organization of Struggle for the Emancipation of the Working Class) in 1978.[^cite_note-95] From 1973 to 1979, the Muslim MEK including [Massoud Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massoud_Rajavi) were mainly in prison.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989152-96] "Rajavi, upon release from prison during the revolution, had to rebuild the organization".[^cite_note-cfr-97][^cite_note-98] In May 1972, there was an assassination attempt on Brig. Gen. Harold Price in Tehran, and in 1973 Lt. Col. [Louis Lee Hawkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Lee_Hawkins) was shot dead in Tehran, though it remains unclear whether the MEK itself or its Marxist offshoot were responsible.[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982141–142-100][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson200980-101][^cite_note-Gibson-102][^cite_note-103][^cite_note-Ash11-104][^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106][^cite_note-state.gov-107][^cite_note-Infobase_Publishing-108][^cite_note-crt-109]

Between 1973 and 1975, the Marxist–Leninist offshoot escalated their militant activities in Iran. In 1973, they engaged in two street battles with Tehran police and bombed ten buildings including Plan Organization, [Pan-American Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-American_Airlines), [Shell Oil Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shell_Oil_Company), Hotel International, Radio City Cinema, and an export company owned by a [Baháʼí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith) businessman. In February 1974, they attacked a police station in Isfahan and in April, they bombed a reception hall, Oman Bank, gates of the British embassy, and offices of Pan-American Oil company in protest of the Sultan of Oman's state visit. A communiqué by the organisation declared that their actions had been to show [solidarity with the people of Dhofar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhofar_Rebellion). On 19 April 1974, they attempted to bomb the SAVAK centre at Tehran University. On 25 May, they set off bombs at three multinational corporations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982141–142-100] In August 1976, a car carrying three American employees of [Rockwell International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockwell_International) - William Cottrell, Donald Smith, and Robert Krongard - was attacked, resulting in their deaths. While some sources suggest the MEK was responsible,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson200956-110] the Marxist offshoot, which at the time had retained the organisation's name, claimed responsibility for the killings in their "Military Communique No.24", concluding that the murders were in retaliation for recent death sentences.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989145–166-111]

### 1979 Iranian Revolution and subsequent power struggles

Main article: [Iranian Revolution](347268.md)

The MEK supported the revolution in its initial phases,[^cite_note-twquarterly-112] and became "a major force in Iranian politics" according to [Ervand Abrahamian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ervand_Abrahamian).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian19891-113] However, it soon entered into conflict with Khomeini,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989171-172-114] and became a leading opposition to the new theocratic regime.[^cite_note-Iran_MEK_Albania-115] By early 1979, the MEK had organised themselves and recreated armed cells, especially in Tehran and helped overthrow the Pahlavi regime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Hern201227–28-116] In January 1979, Massoud Rajavi was released from prison and rebuilt the MEK together with other members that had been imprisoned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Hern201227–28-116][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989171-172-114] Also in January 1979 the MEK released a programme advocating for increased rights for ethnic minorities in Iran, the introduction of welfare-state policies, and gender equality; while the Khomeini regime perceived these demands as a threat.[^cite_note-auto15-117]

Its candidate for the head of the newly founded [Council of Experts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Experts) was [Massoud Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massoud_Rajavi) in the referendum of August 1979. He was not elected.[^cite_note-twquarterly-112] The MEK further launched an unsuccessful campaign supporting the total abolition of Iran's standing military, the [Islamic Republic of Iran Army](48259216.md), in order to prevent a [coup d'état](25914154.md) against the system. They also claimed credit for infiltration against the [Nojeh coup plot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nojeh_coup_plot).[^cite_note-auto30-118] The MEK was one of the supporters of the [occupation of the American embassy in Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis) after the Iranian revolution, although MEK has denied this.[^cite_note-hostage-crisis-support-119]

The MEK refused to participate in the [December 1979 Iranian constitutional referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/December_1979_Iranian_constitutional_referendum) organised by the [Islamic Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republican_Party) to ratify the Constitution drafted by the Assembly of Experts,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989197-120] arguing that the new constitution had failed in many aspects "most important of all, accept the concept of the 'classless tawhidi society'".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989197-120] Despite the opposition, the 3 December 1979 referendum vote approved the new constitution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Hern2012208-1][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989197-120] Once the constitution had been ratified, the MEK proposed Rajavi as their presidential candidate. In his campaign, Rajavi promised to rectify the constitution's shortcomings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989197-120] The conflict surrounding the Constitution intensified when the [Assembly of Experts](512725.md) added numerous clauses that transferred sovereignty from the Iranian population to the [ulama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama), shifting the power to senior clerics and away from the president and elected representatives. In the years that followed, the clerics strengthened their grip on the republic, eventually gaining control over all branches of government and fully establishing a theocratic state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian198955-69-121] As a result of the boycott, Khomeini subsequently refused to allow [Massoud Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massoud_Rajavi) and MEK members to run in the [1980 Iranian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Iranian_presidential_election).[^cite_note-122][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen200915-123] Khomeini declared that "those who had failed to endorse the Constitution could not be trusted to abide by that Constitution".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989198-33] In the March and April 1980 parliamentary elections, the MEK secured the second-highest number of votes. Massoud Rajavi garnered 500,000 votes, while his wife Maryam received over 250,000. However, Khomeini restricted both of them from entering the parliament (Majles).[^cite_note-auto15-117] Rajavi then allied with Iran's new president, [Abolhassan Banisadr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolhassan_Banisadr), elected in January 1980.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Hern201227–28-116]

### Cultural revolution, Iranian protests, and subsequent oppression (1980–1981)

Main articles: [Cultural Revolution in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution_in_Iran), [1981–1982 Iran Massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981%E2%80%931982_Iran_Massacres), [20 June 1981 Iranian protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20_June_1981_Iranian_protests), and [Aftermath of the Iranian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_of_the_Iranian_Revolution)

On June 14, 1980, [Ayatollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah_Khomeini) initiated an order aimed to "purify" higher education by removing Western, liberal, and leftist elements, leading to the closure of universities, the banning of student unions, and violent occupations of campuses. Following the 1979 revolution, the MEK started to gain popularity among university students. During the [Cultural Revolution in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution_in_Iran), clerics imposed policies to Islamise Iranian society, including the expulsion of critical academics, the suppression of secular political groups, and the persecution of intellectuals and artists. These measures sparked large-scale protests across the country.[^cite_note-10.1080_14623528.2022.2105027-124][^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126]

On the final day of the elections, Rajavi met with President [Abolhassan Banisadr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolhassan_Banisadr), complaining that the IRP and its Hezbollah supporters were systematically intimidating voters, disrupting rallies, assaulting campaign workers, and setting ballot boxes on fire. The MEK then arrived at two key conclusions: first, that they had enough popular backing to serve as an opposition to the IRP; and second, that the IRP would not allow them to operate as an opposition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989204–205-127] The group began clashing with the ruling [Islamic Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republican_Party) while avoiding direct and open criticism of Khomeini.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZabih1988250-6] The MEK was in turn suppressed by Khomeini's revolutionary organisations.[^cite_note-128]

In response to the widely disputed impeachment of President Banisadr, the MEK organised a [large-scale protest against Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20_June_1981_Iranian_protests) on June 20, 1981, intending to topple the regime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989218,_219-129] Big crowds gathered in various cities, with the Tehran protest alone attracting up to 500,000 people. Leading clerics proclaimed that demonstrators would be considered "enemies of God" and face immediate execution regardless of age. This marked the beginning of the [1981–1982 Iran Massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981%E2%80%931982_Iran_Massacres) led by the Islamic government.[^cite_note-10.1080_14623528.2022.2105027-124][^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131][^cite_note-\:4-46] In the area around Tehran University, 50 people were killed, 200 wounded, and 1,000 taken into custody, surpassing the intensity of most street battles during the Islamic Revolution. 23 demonstrators were also executed by firing squads, with teenage girls among those executed. From June 24 to 27, the regime executed an additional 50 people. The reported number of executions increased to "600 by September, 1700 by October, and 2500 by December." Initially, the regime publicly displayed the bodies and took pride in declaring the execution of entire families, "including teenage daughters and 60-year-old grandmothers."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989206-207,219-220-132][^cite_note-merat2018-38][^cite_note-Katz=boycott-35] The MEK responded by declaring war against the [Government of Islamic Republic of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Islamic_Republic_of_Iran),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001212-133] and initiating a series of bombings and assassinations targeting the clerical leadership.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZabih1988250-6]

In September 1980 during Iraq's invasion of Iran, the MEK stepped up to fight for their country despite its strained relationship with Khomeini's government. Thousands of MEK members joined the front lines.[^cite_note-134]

#### Hafte Tir bombing

Main article: [Hafte Tir bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafte_Tir_bombing)

On 28 June 1981, the [Islamic Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republican_Party) headquarters was bombed in the [Hafte Tir bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafte_Tir_bombing), which killed 74 party officials and other party members, including [Mohammad Beheshti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Beheshti), the party's secretary-general and [Chief Justice of Iran](35323582.md), 4 [cabinet ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Iran), 10 vice ministers and 27 members of the [Parliament of Iran](383276.md).[^cite_note-hrq204-135][^cite_note-Chronologies-136] Iranian officials initially blamed various groups including the Iraqi government, SAVAK, and the United States.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989220-221-137][^cite_note-138] Two days after the incident [Ruhollah Khomeini](26232.md) accused the MEK.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Hern201232-139] In the years that followed, others were also held accountable, including a man named Mehdi Tafari executed by a Tehran tribunal for his alleged involvement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989218-221-140][^cite_note-141] Kenneth Katzman notes there is much speculation among academics and observers that the bombings could have been orchestrated by top IRP officials as a strategy to eliminate political opponents within the government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001101-53] According to the [United States Department of State](31975.md),[^cite_note-142] in addition to other sources,[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145] the bombing was carried out by the MEK. Ervand Abrahamian argues that whatever the truth may be, the Islamic Republic used this incident to fight the MEK. The MEK declared that the bombing was a "natural and necessary reaction to the regime's atrocities",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989218-221-140] and it never claimed responsibility for the attack.[^cite_note-146]

#### Open conflict with the Islamic Republican Party



Bomb debris after assassination of President [Mohammad-Ali Rajaei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad-Ali_Rajaei) and Prime Minister [Mohammad-Javad Bahonar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad-Javad_Bahonar) in 1981

Further information: [1981 Iranian Prime Minister's office bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_Iranian_Prime_Minister's_office_bombing) and [List of people assassinated by the People's Mujahedin of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_assassinated_by_the_People's_Mujahedin_of_Iran)

While Khomeini and the MEK had allied against the Shah, Khomeini "disliked the MEK's philosophy, which combined Marxist theories of social evolution and class struggle with a view of Shiite Islam that suggested Shiite clerics had misinterpreted Islam and had been collaborators with the ruling class",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Hern201227–28-116] and by mid-1980, clerics close to Khomeini were openly referring to the MEK as "*[monafeghin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munafiqun)*", "*[kafer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafir)*", and "*elteqatigari*".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989206-147] The MEK in turn accused Khomeini and the clerics of "monopolizing power", "hijacking the revolution", "trampling over democratic rights", and "plotting to set up a fascistic one-party dictatorship".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001100-32]

In July 1981, the MEK then formed the [National Council of Resistance of Iran](1181356.md) (NCRI) with the stated goal of uniting the opposition to the Iranian government under one [umbrella organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umbrella_organization).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson200959–60-148] Rajavi assumed the position of chairman of the organisation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiazza199413–14-149] On 30 August 1981, they [bombed the Prime Minister's office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_Iranian_Prime_Minister's_office_bombing), killing the elected President [Rajai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad-Ali_Rajai) and Premier [Mohammad Javad Bahonar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Javad_Bahonar). Iranian authorities announced that Massoud Keshmiri, an MEK member was probably responsible.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2001242–3-150][^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153] The reaction to the Hafte Tir bombing and the bombing of the Prime Minister's office was intense, with many arrests and executions of Mojahedin.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2001243-154] The MEK responded by targeting key Iranian official figures for assassination, as well as attacking low-ranking civil servants and members of the [Revolutionary Guards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IRGC), along with ordinary citizens who supported the new government.[^cite_note-Terrornomics-155]

Between June 1981 and April 1982, around 3,500 MEK members were either executed or killed by the [Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps](1285472.md). Another 5000 MEK prisoners were detained in [camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_camp), and another 8,000 were imprisoned for charges such as possessing copies of Mujahid newspaper. During the same period the MEK was responsible for about 65 percent of nearly 1,000 Khomeini officials killed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZabih1988253-156] From 26 August 1981 to December 1982, the MEK orchestrated 336 attacks against Khomeini officials.[^cite_note-157] In July 1982, 13 IRGC members and [Mohammad Sadoughi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Sadoughi) were killed by MEK members.[^cite_note-merat2018-38]

### Exile and underground opposition activity (1982–1988)

In 1982, the Islamic Republic cracked down MEK operations within Iran.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiazza199414-87] On 8 February [Mousa Khiabani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mousa_Khiabani), Rajavi's deputy and the MEK's field commander in Iran was killed following a three-hour gunfight at a North Tehran safehouse.[^cite_note-\:3-158] Alongside him died his wife Azar Rezaei, [Ashraf Rabiei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashraf_Rabiei), Rajavi's first wife and six others. Rajavi's son Mostafa survived and was later sent to Paris.[^cite_note-Sage-159][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZabih1988253–254-160] The MEK stressed the significance of ideology, which was shaped by its interpretation of what was missing in Iran at the time such as lack of freedom and human rights limitations by the Islamic Republic.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen200923-23] The majority of the MEK leadership and members fled to France, where it operated until 1985.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001101–102-161] In 1983, the MEK started an alliance with Iraq following a meeting between [Massoud Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massoud_Rajavi) and [Tariq Aziz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tariq_Aziz).[^cite_note-162] Iran's deposed president [Abolhassan Banisadr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolhassan_Banisadr) ended his alliance with the group in 1984, denouncing its stance during the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001104–105-163]

In 1986, after French Prime Minister [Jacques Chirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Chirac) struck a deal with Tehran for the release of French hostages held prisoners by the Hezbollah in Lebanon.[^cite_note-164] Also in June 1986, the Islamic Republic "won another major victory in its campaign to isolate the Mojahedin" by persuading the French government to close down the MEK headquarters in Paris. This improved relations between France and Iran. During this period other European nations declined to offer political asylum to the group. With no alternative available and a desire to maintain the group's cohesion, they ultimately decided to relocate to Iraq. James Piazza contends that the MEK's expulsion from France and relocation to Iraq is a "crucial episode" in the group's exile, as it appears Khomeini aimed to send the MEK to a remote place. However, the group ended up in a location that enabled it to continue its cross-border attacks. MEK representatives contend that their organisation had little alternative to moving to Iraq considering its aim of toppling the Iranian clerical government.[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-auto23-59] By 1987, most MEK leaders were based in Iraq, where the group remained until the [2003 US invasion](201936.md). According to the US State Department, the MEK was mainly supported by Iraq during that period and was fighting on the Iraqi side in the 1980–1988 [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War).[^cite_note-166]

From 1982 to 1988, despite the mounting casualties on both sides, the lingering underground presence of the MEK in Iran remained operational and went on to perform an average of sixty operations per week, resulting in assassinations of important Khomeini deputies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZabih1988253–254-160] The MEK came to be considered Iran's "largest and most active Iranian exile organization",[^cite_note-auto6-167][^cite_note-168][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian19891-113] and its publications were commonly circulated within the Iranian diaspora.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZabih1988256-169]

#### Operations Shining Sun, Forty Stars, and Eternal Light

Further information: [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War), [Operation Mersad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Mersad), and [Operation Forty Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Forty_Stars)



National Liberation Army of Iran MD-500



MEK leader Massoud Rajavi with Iraqi president [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein).

The MEK's official argument for moving to Iraq was that it would place them geographically close to their enemy, the [Islamic Republic government in Iran](50913538.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2009\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_February_2025\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (February_2025)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-170][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989258-171] In 1987 Masoud Rajavi declared the establishment of the **National Liberation Army of Iran** ([Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language): ارتش آزادیبخش ملی ایران, [romanised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Persian): *Arteš-e Âzâdibaxš-e Melli-e Irân*, **NLA**). It served as an infantry force that included different militant groups and members of the NCRI. its sole objective was to "overthrow the Islamic Republic using a military force outside the country."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2018-60][^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173] Through a broadcast on Baghdad radio, the MEK extended an invitation to all progressive-nationalist Iranian individuals to join the NLA in overthrowing the government of the Islamic Republic.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiazza199420-174]

On 27 March 1988, the NLA launched its first military offensive against the Islamic Republic's armed forces.[^cite_note-\:1-64] The NLA captured 600 square-kilometres of Islamic Republic territory and 508 soldiers from the Iranian 77th infantry division in [Khuzestan Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khuzestan_Province).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiazza199422-175] The operation was named "Shining Sun"[^cite_note-\:0-63][^cite_note-\:1-64][^cite_note-\:2-65][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2018-60] (or "Operation Bright Sun")[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiazza199422-175] in which according to Massoud Rajavi, 2000 soldiers of the Islamic Republic were killed and \$100 million worth of equipment was captured and exhibited for journalists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiazza199422-175]

[Operation Forty Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Forty_Stars) was launched on June 18, 1988. With 530 aircraft sorties and heavy use of [nerve gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarin), they attacked to the Iranian forces in the area around Mehran, killing or wounding 3,500 and nearly destroying a Revolutionary Guard division. The forces captured the city and took positions in the heights near Mehran, coming close to wiping out the whole Iranian Pasdaran division and taking most of its equipment.[^cite_note-csis10-176] While some sources claim that Iraq participated in the operation,[^cite_note-hup454-177] the MEK and Baghdad said Iraqi soldiers did not take part.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179]

Near the end of the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War), a military force of 7,000 members of the MEK, armed and equipped by Saddam's Iraq and calling itself the National Liberation Army of Iran (NLA) was founded.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson20093-180] On 26 July 1988, six days after Ayatollah Khomeini had announced his acceptance of the UN-brokered ceasefire resolution, the NLA advanced under heavy Iraqi air cover, crossing the Iranian border from Iraq.[^cite_note-181] It seized the Iranian town of [Islamabad-e Gharb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamabad-e_Gharb). As it advanced further into Iran, Iraq ceased its air support, with Iranian forces cutting off NLA supply lines and counterattacking under cover of fighter planes and helicopter gunships. The MEK claims it lost 1,400 dead or missing and the Islamic Republic sustained 55,000 casualties. It claims to have killed 4,500 NLA during the operation.[^cite_note-182] The operation was called *Foroughe Javidan* (Eternal Light) by the MEK and the counterattack [Operation Mersad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Mersad) by the Iranian forces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001102-183] Rajavi later stated that "the failure of Eternal Light was not a military blunder, but was instead rooted in the members' thoughts for their spouses".[^cite_note-merat2018-38]

#### 1988 execution of MEK prisoners

Main article: [1988 executions of Iranian political prisoners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_executions_of_Iranian_political_prisoners)

Following the MEK's [Operation Mersad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Mersad) against Iranian forces, thousands of imprisoned members of the MEK, along with members of other leftist opposition groups, [were executed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_executions_of_Iranian_prisoners).[^cite_note-The_Bloody_Red_Summer-184][^cite_note-Montazeri01-185] The Iranian government used the MEK's failed invasion as a pretext for the mass execution of those "who remained steadfast in their support for the MEK" and other jailed opposition group members.[^cite_note-auto29-186][^cite_note-merat2018-38] Most of the prisoners executed were there for nonviolent activities like distributing newspapers and leaflets, joining protests, or raising money for the opposition. Some were imprisoned for holding outlawed political views.[^cite_note-BS-187]<sup>: 11 </sup>

On 19 July 1988, the authorities isolated major prisons, having its courts of law go on an unscheduled holiday to prevent relatives from inquiring about those imprisoned,[^cite_note-Abrahamian_1999_209–214-188] and as [Ervand Abrahamian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ervand_Abrahamian) notes, "thus began an act of violence unprecedented in Iranian history". Prisoners were asked if they were willing to denounce the MEK before cameras, help the IRI hunt down MEK members and name secret sympathisers. Those who gave unsatisfactory answers were promptly taken away and hanged.[^cite_note-Abrahamian_1999_209–214-188] Some women and youths were among those executed.[^cite_note-189] Human rights groups say that the number of those executed remains uncertain, but "thousands of political dissidents were systematically subjected to enforced disappearance in Iranian detention facilities across the country",[^cite_note-auto29-186][^cite_note-auto1-190] with those executed charged with "moharebeh" or "waging war on God",[^cite_note-auto2-191] and of "disclosing state secrets" and threatening national security".[^cite_note-auto29-186] Since the executions, [Amnesty International](18947898.md) has stated that "there has also been an ongoing campaign by the Islamic Republic to demonize victims, distort facts, and repress family survivors and human rights defenders."[^cite_note-auto31-192][^cite_note-193] Under international law, these mass extrajudicial killings without fair proceedings were also considered systematic crimes against humanity.[^cite_note-194]

The executions were ordered directly by [Ayatollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah_Khomeini) through a written [fatwa](11259.md). The killings were implemented by "death committees," composed of an Islamic judge, a representative of the Ministry of Intelligence, and a state prosecutor. High-ranking officials, such as [Mohammad Khatami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Khatami) and [Ayatollah Ali Khamenei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah_Ali_Khamenei), were identified by opposition groups as responsible.[^cite_note-sotoudeh-195][^cite_note-196]

According to Professor Cheryl Bernard, the mass execution of political prisoners carried out by the Islamic Republic in 1981 caused the MEK to split into four groups: those that were arrested, imprisoned or executed, a group that went underground in Iran, another that left to Kurdistan and a final group that left to other countries abroad.[^cite_note-197] By the end of 1981, the principal refuge for many exiled members of the MEK had become France.[^cite_note-Final_Judgment-198]

### Post-war Saddam era (1988–2003)

See also: [1991 uprisings in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_uprisings_in_Iraq)

The Iranian government is believed to be concerned about MEK activities in Iran. As such, MEK supporters are a major target of Iran's internal security apparatus abroad[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman20014,104-199][^cite_note-auto5-200] and it is said to be responsible for killing MEK members, [Kazem Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazem_Rajavi) on 24 April 1990 and [Mohammad-Hossein Naghdi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Hossein_Naghdi), a NCRI representative on 6 March 1993.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman20014,104-199] In 1991 the MEK was accused of helping the Iraqi Republican Guard suppress Shiite and Kurdish [nationwide uprisings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Iraqi_uprisings), a claim the MEK has consistently denied.[^cite_note-bdt45cgf112-73][^cite_note-aph.gov.au-201] Ervand Abrahamian suggests that one motivation for the MEK's opposition to the clerical regime was its infringement on the rights of national minorities, especially the Kurds.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989208-202]

In April 1992, the MEK attacked 10 Iranian embassies, including the Iranian Mission to the United Nations in New York using different weapons, taking hostages, and injuring Iranian ambassadors and embassy employees. There were dozens of arrests.[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-NYT-missions-204] According to MEK representatives, the attacks were a way to protest the bombing of a MEK military base where several people had been killed and wounded.[^cite_note-NYT-missions-204]

In June 1998 [FIFA](11049.md) president [Sepp Blatter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sepp_Blatter) said that he received "anonymous threats of disruption from Iranian exiles" for the [1998 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_FIFA_World_Cup) match between [Iran](272865.md) and the [U.S.](582488.md) football teams at [Stade de Gerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stade_de_Gerland).[^cite_note-205] The MEK bought some 7,000 out of 42,000 tickets for the match between, in order to promote themselves with the political banners they smuggled. The plan was ultimately foiled with TV cameras avoiding filming them, and intelligence sources having been tipped off about a potential [pitch invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pitch_invasion). To prevent an interruption in the match, extra security entered Stade Gerland.[^cite_note-206]

In 1999, after a 2 1⁄2-year investigation, Federal authorities arrested 29 individuals in Operation Eastern Approach,[^cite_note-CNN-207] of whom 15 were held on charges of helping MEK members illegally enter the United States.[^cite_note-Rosenzweig-208] The ringleader pled guilty to providing fake documents to MEK members and to violations of the [Antiterrorism and Effective Death Penalty Act of 1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiterrorism_and_Effective_Death_Penalty_Act_of_1996).[^cite_note-articles.latimes.com-209][^cite_note-Californian_pleads_guilty_to_aiding-210] In 2002 the [NCRI](1181356.md) publicly called or the formation of a National Solidarity Front to help overthrow the Iranian Government.[^cite_note-211]

#### 2003 French arrests

In June 2003, French police raided the MEK's properties, including its base in [Auvers-sur-Oise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auvers-sur-Oise), under the orders of anti-terrorist magistrate [Jean-Louis Bruguière](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Louis_Brugui%C3%A8re), after suspicions that it was trying to shift its base of operations there. 160 suspected MEK members were then arrested, including [Maryam Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryam_Rajavi) and her brother Saleh Rajavi.[^cite_note-212] After questioning, most of those detained were released, but 24 members, including [Maryam Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryam_Rajavi), were kept in detention.[^cite_note-213]

In response, 40 supporters began [hunger strikes](222292.md) to protest the arrests, and 10 members including [Neda Hassani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neda_Hassani), [immolated](916669.md) themselves in various European capitals.[^cite_note-hunger-214][^cite_note-sp-online-215] French Interior Minister [Nicolas Sarkozy](557007.md) declared that the MEK "recently wanted to make France its support base, notably after the intervention in Iraq", while [Pierre de Bousquet de Florian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_de_Bousquet_de_Florian), head of France's domestic intelligence service, claimed that the group was "transforming its [Val d'Oise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val_d'Oise) centre \[near Paris] \[...] into an international terrorist base".[^cite_note-hunger-214] Police found \$1.3 million in [\$100 bills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_one_hundred-dollar_bill) in cash in their offices.[^cite_note-216]

U.S. Senator [Sam Brownback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Brownback), a Republican from Kansas and chairman of the Foreign Relations subcommittee on South Asia, then accused the French of doing "the Iranian government's dirty work". Along with other members of [Congress](31756.md), he wrote a letter of protest to President [Jacques Chirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Chirac), while longtime MEK supporters such as [Sheila Jackson Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheila_Jackson_Lee), a Democrat from Texas, criticised Maryam Radjavi's arrest.[^cite_note-Rubin-217] A court later found that there were no grounds for terrorism or terrorism-related finance charges.[^cite_note-218] In 2014, prosecuting judges also dropped all charges of money laundering and fraud.[^cite_note-219]

### Post-U.S. invasion of Iraq (2003–2016)



Entrance Gate of Ashraf City when populated by PMOI exiles

In May 2003, during the [Iraq War](5043324.md), the [Coalition forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-National_Force_%E2%80%93_Iraq) bombed MEK bases and forced them to surrender.[^cite_note-220] This resulted in at least 50 deaths.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-Spencer-221] The US forces then disarmed [Camp Ashraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Ashraf) residents.[^cite_note-disarmament-71] In the operation, the U.S. reportedly captured 6,000 MEK soldiers and over 2,000 pieces of military equipment, including 19 British-made Chieftain tanks.[^cite_note-Sullivan-223][^cite_note-autogenerated1-224] Following the occupation the U.S. did not hand over MEK fighters to Iran.[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson2009xiv,_17-226] The group's core members were for many years effectively confined to Camp Ashraf,[^cite_note-227] before later being relocated to a former U.S. military base, [Camp Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Liberty), in Iraq.[^cite_note-NYT_2012-228] Then-Secretary of Defense Donald Rumsfeld and Vice President Dick Cheney argued that the MEK should be used against Iran.[^cite_note-theguardian.com-229][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson2009xiv,_17-226] They were then placed under the guard of the [U.S. Military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Military). Defectors from the MEK requested assistance from the [Coalition forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-National_Force_%E2%80%93_Iraq), who created a "temporary internment and protection facility" for them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson20095-230] In the first year these numbered "several hundred", mainly Iranian soldiers captured in the Iran–Iraq War and other Iranians lured to the MEK.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson200947-231] In all, during the period of US control, nearly 600 members of the MEK defected.[^cite_note-232]

In June 2004, Donald Rumsfeld designated the MeK as protected persons under the [Fourth Geneva Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Geneva_Convention).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson20095,_41-233][^cite_note-theguardian.com-229][^cite_note-fourth-geneva-convention-234] and signed a formal ceasefire agreement.[^cite_note-disarmament-71] Since 2009, when the Iraqi government became openly hostile to MEK, the U.S. led efforts to get the group's members out of Iraq.[^cite_note-harb2019-235] After it was no longer designated as a terrorist group, the US was able to convince [Albania](738.md) to accept the remaining 2,700 members who were brought to Tirana between 2014 and 2016.[^cite_note-theguardian.com-229][^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237][^cite_note-reuters_2016-09-09-238]

Separate to events in Iraq, the organisation launched a free-to-air satellite [television network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_network) named *Vision of Freedom* ([Sima-ye-Azadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simaye_Azadi)) in England in 2003.[^cite_note-239] It previously operated *Vision of Resistance* analogue television in Iraq in the 1990s, accessible in western provinces of Iran.[^cite_note-240] They also had a radio station, *Radio Iran Zamin*, that was closed down in June 1998.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarmonBowdish20188–9,_12,_14-241] In 2006, an EU freeze on the group's funds was overturned by the [European Court of First Instance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_of_First_Instance).[^cite_note-242] In 2010 and 2011 [Ali Saremi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Ali_Saremi),[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245] [Mohammad Ali Haj Aghaei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Ali_Haj_Aghaei) and [Jafar Kazemi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jafar_Kazemi) were executed by the Iranian government for co-operating with the MEK.[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247]

#### Iraqi government crackdown (2009–2013)

See also: [2011 raid on Camp Ashraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_raid_on_Camp_Ashraf) and [2013 Camp Ashraf massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Camp_Ashraf_massacre)

In 2009 American troops gave the Iraqi government responsibility of the MEK. Iraqi authorities, which were sympathetic to Iran, allowed Iran-linked militias to attack the MEK.[^cite_note-Iran_MEK_Albania-115] Prime Minister [Nuri al-Maliki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuri_al-Maliki) announced that the group would not be allowed to base its operations from Iraqi soil.[^cite_note-248] On 23 January 2009, while on a visit to Tehran, Iraqi National Security Advisor Mowaffak al-Rubaie reiterated the Iraqi Prime Minister's earlier announcement that the MEK organisation would no longer be able to base itself on Iraqi soil and stated that the members of the organisation would have to make a choice, either to go back to Iran or to go to a third country, adding that these measures would be implemented over the next two months.[^cite_note-249]

On 28 July 2009, Iraqi security forces raided MEK headquarters at [Camp Ashraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Ashraf). MEK claimed 11 dead and 400 injured in clashes while the Iraqi government claimed 30 policemen injured.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251] U.S. officials had long opposed a violent takeover of the camp northeast of Baghdad, and the raid is thought to symbolise the declining American influence in Iraq.[^cite_note-252] After the raid, the U.S. Secretary of State, [Hillary Rodham Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Rodham_Clinton), stated the issue was "completely within \[the Iraqi government's] purview".[^cite_note-253] In the course of attack, 36 Iranian dissidents were arrested and removed from the camp to a prison in a town named Khalis, where the arrestees went on hunger strike for 72 days. Finally, the dissidents were released when they were in an extremely critical condition and on the verge of death.[^cite_note-254]

In January 2010, Iranian authorities charged five MEK [protesters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Iranian_presidential_election_protests) of "rioting and arson" under the crime of *[moharebeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hirabah#Iran)*, an offence reserved for those who "take up arms against the state" and carries the death penalty.[^cite_note-255] In July 2010, the [Supreme Iraqi Criminal Tribunal](766565.md) issued an arrest warrant for 39 MEK members, including [Massoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massoud_Rajavi) and [Maryam Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryam_Rajavi), accusing them of [crimes against humanity](61099.md) during the [1991 uprisings in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_uprisings_in_Iraq). The MEK denied the charges.[^cite_note-cah-256]

In 2012, the MEK moved from Camp Ashraf to [Camp Hurriya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Hurriya) in [Baghdad](4492.md) (a onetime U.S. base formerly known as [Camp Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Liberty)). A rocket and mortar attack killed 5 and injured 50 others at Camp Hurriya on 9 February 2013. MEK residents of the facility and their representatives appealed to the [UN Secretary-General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Secretary-General) and U.S. officials to let them return to Ashraf, which they said has concrete buildings and shelters that offer more protection. The United States has been working with the [UN High Commissioner for Refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_High_Commissioner_for_Refugees) on the resettlement project.[^cite_note-257]

In 2013, 52 unarmed MEK members were killed during an attack on Camp Ashraf. 7 other members were also reported missing. Iraqi security forces are thought to be responsible for the assault, with guidance and support from the Iranian government.[^cite_note-258]

#### Iran's nuclear programme

See also: [Nuclear program of Iran](721807.md), [Timeline of the nuclear program of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_nuclear_program_of_Iran), [Assassination of Iranian nuclear scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Iranian_nuclear_scientists), and [Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Comprehensive_Plan_of_Action)

The MEK and the NCRI revealed the existence of [Iran's nuclear programme](721807.md) in a press conference held on 14 August 2002 in Washington, D.C. MEK representative [Alireza Jafarzadeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alireza_Jafarzadeh) stated that Iran is running two top-secret projects, one in the city of [Natanz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natanz) and another in [a facility located in Arak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IR-40), which was later confirmed by the [International Atomic Energy Agency](14984.md).[^cite_note-Porter-259][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFayazmanesh2008120–123-260]

Journalists [Seymour Hersh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seymour_Hersh) and [Connie Bruck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connie_Bruck) have written that the information was given to the MEK by Israel.[^cite_note-Gareth_Porter-261] Among others, it was described by a senior IAEA official and a monarchist advisor to [Reza Pahlavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Pahlavi,_Crown_Prince_of_Iran), who said before MEK they were offered to reveal the information, but they refused because it would be seen negatively by the people of Iran.[^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263] Similar accounts could be found elsewhere by others, including comments made by US officials.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFayazmanesh2008120–123-260]

On 18 November 2004, MEK representative Mohammad Mohaddessin used satellite images to state that a new facility existed in northeast [Tehran](57654.md) named "Center for the Development of Advanced Defence Technology". This allegation by MEK and all their subsequent allegations were false.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFayazmanesh2008120–123-260]

In 2010 the NCRI claimed to have uncovered a secret nuclear facility in Iran. These claims were dismissed by U.S. officials, who did not believe the facilities to be nuclear. In 2013, the NCRI again claimed to have discovered a secret underground nuclear site.[^cite_note-reuters-264]

In 2012, NBC News' Richard Engel and Robert Windrem published a report quoting U.S. officials, who spoke to [NBC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC_News) on condition of anonymity, that the MEK was being "financed, trained, and armed by [Israel's secret service](27619007.md)" to assassinate Iranian nuclear scientists.[^cite_note-rockcente-265][^cite_note-266] A senior U.S. State Department official said the Department never claimed that the MEK was involved in the assassinations of Iranian nuclear scientists.[^cite_note-267] Former CIA [case officer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case_officer) in the Middle East, [Robert Baer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Baer) said that the perpetrators "could only be Israel", and that "it is quite likely Israel is acting in tandem with" the MEK.[^cite_note-268]

On 27 November 2020, Iran's top nuclear scientist [Mohsen Fakhrizadeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohsen_Fakhrizadeh) was assassinated. Iranian Rear Admiral [Ali Shamkhani](262775.md), who heads the Supreme National Security Council, blamed Mujahideen-e-Khalq and Israel.[^cite_note-269]

### Settlement in Albania (2016–present)

In 2016, the United States brokered a deal to relocate the MEK to [Albania](738.md). About 3,000 members moved to Albania, and the U.S. donated \$20 million to the U.N. refugee agency to help them resettle.[^cite_note-270] On 9 September 2016, more than 280 remaining MEK members were relocated to Albania.[^cite_note-reuters_2016-09-09-238] [Camp Ashraf 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Ashraf_3) is located in [Manëz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man%C3%ABz), [Durrës County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durr%C3%ABs_County), where they have been protested by the locals.[^cite_note-durres-locals-5] While the level of support for the MEK in Iran remains unclear, Iranian state media reported in July 2026 that a number of people linked to the group had been executed.[^cite_note-271]

#### Relationship during Trump presidency

In 2017, the year before [John Bolton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_R._Bolton) became President Trump's National Security Adviser, Bolton addressed members of the MEK and said that they would celebrate in Tehran before 2019.[^cite_note-272] By 2018, operatives of the MEK were believed to be still conducting [covert operations](613453.md) inside Iran to overthrow Iran's government.[^cite_note-273] It also maintained some operations in France, and in January 2018, Iranian President [Hassan Rouhani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_Rouhani) phoned French president [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md), asking him to order kicking the MEK out of its base in [Auvers-sur-Oise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auvers-sur-Oise), alleging that the MEK stirred up the [2017–18 Iranian protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%9318_Iranian_protests).[^cite_note-274] By 2018, over 4,000 MEK members had entered Albania, according to the [INSTAT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INSTAT) data.[^cite_note-275]

On 30 June 2018, Rudy Giuliani, Donald Trump's personal lawyer, lectured an MEK gathering in Paris, calling for regime change in Tehran. John McCain and John Bolton have met the MEK's leader Maryam Rajavi or spoken at its rallies.[^cite_note-ipgbu-276][^cite_note-Merat_2018-277]



John Bolton speaking at a MEK event

During the Free Iran 2019 conference in Albania, former New York City mayor [Rudy Giuliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudy_Giuliani) attended an MEK podium, where he described the group as a "government-in-exile", saying it is a ready-to-go alternative to lead the country if the Iranian government falls.[^cite_note-harb2019-235] Additionally, the [Trump administration](52231341.md) said it would not rule out the MEK as a viable replacement for the current Iranian regime.[^cite_note-278]

#### Islamic Republic of Iran operations against MEK inside Europe

See also: [Iranian diplomat terror plot trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_diplomat_terror_plot_trial), [Iran and state-sponsored terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_and_state-sponsored_terrorism), and [Albania–Iran relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albania%E2%80%93Iran_relations)

On 30 June 2018 [Belgian police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_police) arrested married couple of Iranian heritage Amir Saadouni and Nasimeh Naami on charges of "attempted terrorist murder and preparing a terrorist act" against an MEK rally in France. The couple had in their possession half of a kilogram of TATP explosives and a detonator. Police also detained [Asadollah Asadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asadollah_Asadi), an Iranian diplomat in Vienna. German prosecutors charged Asadi with "activity as foreign agent and conspiracy to commit murder by contacting the couple and giving them a device containing 500 grams of TATP". Prosecutors said Asadi was a member of the Iranian Ministry of Intelligence and Security service, an organisation that focuses on "combating of opposition groups inside and outside of Iran".[^cite_note-Steven2-279][^cite_note-auto3-280][^cite_note-281] Iran responded that the arrests were a "[false flag ploy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_flag)", with the Iranian Foreign Ministry spokesman saying the "two suspects in Belgium were in fact members of the People's Mujahideen".[^cite_note-282] In October 2018, the French government officially and publicly blamed Iran's Intelligence Service for the failed attack against the MEK. U.S. officials also condemned Iran over the foiled bomb plot that France blames on Tehran.[^cite_note-283] In December 2018, Albania expelled two Iranian diplomats due to alleged involvement in the bomb plot against the MEK (where Mayor Giuliani and other US government officials were also gathered) accusing the two of "violating their diplomatic status".[^cite_note-auto9-284][^cite_note-285] Iranian President [Hassan Rouhani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_Rouhani) said that the MEK incited violence during the [2017–2018 Iranian protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%932018_Iranian_protests).[^cite_note-286]

In October 2019, Albanian police discovered an Iranian paramilitary network that allegedly planned attacks against MEK members in Albania. Albania's police chief, Ardi Veliu, said that the Iran Revolutionary Guard's foreign wing operated an "active terrorist cell" that targeted members of the MEK. A police statement said that two Iranian security officials led the network from Tehran, and that it was allegedly linked to organised crime groups in Turkey. It also said that the network used a former MEK member to collect information in Albania. Valiu also said that a planned attack on the MEK by Iranian government agents was foiled in March.[^cite_note-287]

In 2020, newspaper *[De Standaard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_Standaard)* said evidence that Iranian intelligence and security was involved in the failed 2018 bomb plot against an MEK rally was mounting. In a note to the federal prosecutor's office, the State Security writes that "the attack was devised in the name and under the impetus of Iran", with the note also describing one of the case's suspects, [Asadollah Asadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asadollah_Asadi), as a [MOIS](501536.md) agent. Amir Saadouni and Nasimeh Naami, who in 2018 were found with half a kilo of explosives and are also being charged in the case, admitted that they had been in contact with Asadollah Asadi.[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-auto3-280] In October 2020, the Iranian diplomat Asadollah Asadi charged in Belgium with planning to bomb a rally by the MEK "warned authorities of possible retaliation by unidentified groups if he is found guilty". Asadi would become the first Iranian diplomat to go on trial on charges of terrorism within the European Union.[^cite_note-289][^cite_note-290] In February 2021, Asadi and his accomplices were found guilty of attempted terrorism and Asadi was sentenced to 20 years in prison.[^cite_note-291]

In September 2022, Albania suffered a second cyber-attack, resulting in it cutting diplomatic ties with the Islamic Republic and ordering Iranian embassy staff to leave.[^cite_note-auto9-284][^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293] According to the [FBI](11127.md) and [CISA](59407369.md), the cyberattacks were motivated by Albania's hosting of the MEK.[^cite_note-294]

In 2023, Deputy Minister of International Affairs of the Judiciary [Kazem Gharibabadi](78777113.md) opened a case against 107 members of the MEK for treason, calling the organisation "a terrorist group whose hands are stained with the blood of thousands of Iranians".[^cite_note-295]
