In the 1960s the MEK created a series of pamphlets designed to outline their worldviews. Their work "The Portrait of a Muslim" is thought to be the "first book in Persian" to systematically interpret "early Shiism as a protest movement against class exploitation and state oppression." The group's early ideology asserted that science, reason, and modernity were compatible with Islam. They adopted the concept of class struggle from [Karl Marx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Marx) but rejected being labeled as Marxists or socialists as they believed in the spiritual dimension of human beings, a concept incompatible with Marxist philosophy. During this period, the MEK's ideology embraced class struggle and historical determinism but rejected the denial of God.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian198992–230-296]

According to Katzman, the MEK's early ideology is a matter of dispute. While scholars generally describe the MEK's ideology as an attempt to combine "[Islam](6037917.md) with revolutionary [Marxism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism)", today the organisation claims that it has always emphasised Islam, and that Marxism and Islam are incompatible. Despite their Marxist influence, the group never used the terms "socialist" or "communist" to describe themselves.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian198981–126-80][^cite_note-ReferenceA-81] Katzman writes that their ideology "espoused the creation of a [classless society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classless_society) that would combat world [imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialism), international [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism), [colonialism](7299.md), exploitation, racism, and multinational corporations".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman200199-18] The MEK's ideological foundation was developed during the period of the Iran revolution. According to its official history, the MEK first defined itself as a group that wanted to establish a nationalist, democratic, revolutionary Muslim organisation in favour of change in Iran.[^cite_note-297]

Historian Ervand Abrahamian observed that the MEK were "consciously influenced by Marxism, both [modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Marxism) and [classical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Marxism)", but they always denied being Marxists because they were aware that the term was colloquial to '[atheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheistic) materialism' among Iran's general public. The Iranian regime for the same reason was "eager to pin on the Mojahedin the labels of [Islamic-Marxists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Marxist) and Marxist-Muslims".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989100–101-298]

According to Abrahamian, it was the first Iranian organisation to develop systematically a modern revolutionary interpretation of Islam that "differed sharply from both the old conservative Islam of the traditional clergy and the new populist version formulated in the 1970s by Ayatollah Khomeini and his disciples".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian19891-113] Abrahamian said that the MEK's early ideology constituted a "combination of Muslim themes; Shii notions of martyrdom; classical Marxist theories of class struggle and historical determinism; and neo-Marxist concepts of armed struggle, guerilla warfare and revolutionary heroism".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989100-299] According to James Piazza, the MEK worked towards the creation by armed popular struggle of a society in which ethnic, gender, or class discrimination would be obliterated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiazza199411-300]

Nasser Sadegh told military tribunals that although the MEK respected Marxism as a "progressive method of social analysis, they could not accept materialism, which was contrary to their Islamic ideology". The MEK eventually had a falling out with Marxist groups. According to Sepehr Zabir, "they soon became Enemy No. 1 of both pro-Soviet Marxist groups, the [Tudeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudeh_Party_of_Iran) and the Majority Fedayeen."[^cite_note-auto30-118]

The MEK's ideology of revolutionary [Shi'ism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelver_Shi'ism) is based on an interpretation of Islam so similar to that of [Ali Shariati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Shariati) that "many concluded" they were inspired by him. According to Ervand Abrahamian, it is clear that "in later years" that Shariati and "his prolific works" had "indirectly helped the Mujahedin".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982490-301]

In the group's "first major ideological work", *Nahzat-i Husseini* or [Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Husayn_ibn_Ali)'s Movement, authored by one of the group's founders, Ahmad Reza'i, it was argued that *Nezam-i Towhid* (monotheistic order) sought by the prophet Muhammad, was a commonwealth fully united not only in its worship of one God but in a classless society that strives for the common good. "Shiism, particularly Hussein's historic act of martyrdom and resistance, has both a revolutionary message and a special place in our popular culture."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982491-302]

As described by Abrahamian, one Mojahedin ideologist argued:

> Reza'i further argued that the banner of revolt raised by the Shi'i [Imams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imamate_\(Twelver_doctrine\)), especially [Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali), [Hassan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_ibn_Ali), and [Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Husayn_ibn_Ali), was aimed against feudal landlords and exploiting merchant capitalists as well as against usurping [Caliphs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate) who betrayed the *Nezam-i-Towhid*. For Reza'i and the Mujahidin it was the duty of all Muslims to continue this struggle to create a '[classless society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classless_society)' and destroy all forms of [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism), [despotism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Despotism), and [imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialism). The Mojahedin summed up their attitude towards religion in these words: 'After years of extensive study into Islamic history and Shi'i ideology, our organization has reached the firm conclusion that Islam, especially Shi'ism, will play a major role in inspiring the masses to join the revolution. It will do so because Shi'ism, particularly [Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Husayn_ibn_Ali)'s historic act of resistance, has both a revolutionary message and a special place in our popular culture.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeddie2006220–221-303]

### After the revolution



MEK demonstrators carrying [Lion and Sun](8702401.md) flags and those of 'National Liberation Army of Iran'.

Massoud Rajavi supported the idea that Shi'ism is compatible with pluralistic democracy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZabih1988253–254-160] In 1981, after signing the "covenant of freedom and independence" with [Banisadr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banisadr), and establishing [NCRI](1181356.md) Massoud Rajavi made an announcement to the foreign press about the MEK's ideology saying that "First we want freedom for all political parties. We reject both political prisoners and political executions. In the true spirit of Islam, we advocate freedom, fraternity, and an end to all repression, censorship, and injustices."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZabih1988252–254-304] They appealed to all opposition groups to join [NCRI](1181356.md). Some secular groups had reservations that an "Islamic Democratic People's Republic" was unattainable, while Massoud Rajavi maintained that Shiite religion and pluralistic democracy are compatible.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZabih1988252–255-305] Along with former Iranian president [Abolhassan Banisadr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolhassan_Banisadr), Rajavi published a Covenant promoting freedom of speech, press, and religion in Iran, as well as protection of Iranian minorities, "especially the Kurdish minority".&#x20;

In 2001, Kenneth Katzman wrote that the MEK had "tried to show itself as worthy of U.S. support on the basis of its commitment to values compatible with those of the United States – democracy, free market economics, protection of the rights of women and minorities, and peaceful relations with Iran's neighbors", but some analysts dispute that they are genuinely committed to what they state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman200199,_107-306] According to Department of State's October 1994 report, the MEK used violence in its campaign to overthrow the Iranian regime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001107-307] A 2009 [U.S. Department of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Department_of_State) report stated that their ideology was a blend of [Marxism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism), [Islamism](15012.md) and [feminism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feminism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarmonBowdish2018170-308]

The MEK says it is seeking regime change in Iran through peaceful means with an aim to replace the clerical rule in Iran with a secular government.[^cite_note-309] It also claims to have disassociated itself from its former revolutionary ideology in favour of liberal democratic values, but they fail to "present any track record to substantiate a capability or intention to be democratic".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClark201673-310]

The MEK says it supports a "secular democratic system", where their leader, [Maryam Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryam_Rajavi), calls for a "pluralist system", a non-nuclear Iran, human rights and freedom of expression, a separation of government and religion, and an end to Sharia law.[^cite_note-311]

### Ideological revolution and women's rights

During the transitional period, the MEK projected an image of a "forward looking, radical and progressive Islamic force". Throughout the revolution, the MEK played a major role in developing the "revolutionary Muslim woman", which was portrayed as "the living example of the new ideal of womanhood".[^cite_note-312] The MEK is "known for its female-led military units".[^cite_note-auto33-313] According to [Ervand Abrahamian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ervand_Abrahamian), the MEK "declared that God had created men and women to be equal in all things: in political and intellectual matters, as well as in legal, economic, and social issues."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989233-314] According to Tohidi, in 1982, as the government in Tehran led an expansive effort to limit women's rights, the MEK adopted a female leadership. In 1987, the National Liberation Army (NLA), "saw female resistors commanding military operations from their former base at Camp Ashraf (in Diyala, Iraq) to Iran's westernmost provinces, where they engaged alongside the men in armed combat with Iran's regular and paramilitary forces".[^cite_note-315][^cite_note-316]

Shortly after the revolution, Rajavi married Ashraf Rabii, an MEK member regarded as "the symbol of revolutionary womanhood".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989181-317] Rabii was killed by Iranian forces in 1982. On 27 January 1985, [Massoud Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massoud_Rajavi) appointed [Maryam Azodanlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryam_Rajavi) as his co-equal leader. The announcement, stated that this would give women equal say within the organisation and thereby "would launch a great ideological revolution within Mojahedin, the Iranian public and the whole Muslim World".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989251–253-318]

In 1985, Rajavi launched an "ideological revolution" banning marriage and enforced divorce on all members who were required to separate from their spouses.[^cite_note-merat2018-38] Five weeks later, the MEK announced that its Politburo and Central Committee had asked Rajavi and Azondalu, who was already married, to marry one another to deepen and pave the way for the "ideological revolution". At the time Maryam Azodanlu was known only as the younger sister of a veteran member, and the wife of [Mehdi Abrishamchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehdi_Abrishamchi). According to the announcement, Maryam Azodanlu and Mehdi Abrishamchi had recently divorced in order to facilitate this 'great revolution.' According to [Ervand Abrahamian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ervand_Abrahamian) "in the eyes of traditionalists, particularly among the bazaar middle class, the whole incident was indecent. It smacked of wife-swapping, especially when Abrishamchi announced his own marriage to Khiabani's younger sister. It involved women with young children and wives of close friends – a taboo in traditional Iranian culture;" something that further isolated the Mojahedin and also upset some members of the organisation. Also according to Abrahamian, "the incident was equally outrageous in the eyes of the secularists, especially among the modern intelligentsia. It projected onto the public arena a matter that should have been treated as a private issue between two individuals."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989251–253-318] Many criticised Maryam Azodanlu's giving up her own maiden name (something most Iranian women did not do and she herself had not done in her previous marriage). They would question whether this was in line with her claims of being a staunch feminist.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989251–253-318]

[Maryam Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryam_Rajavi) became increasingly important over feminism-coloured politics. The emancipation of women is now depicted in Maryam Rajavi's writings "as both a policy end and a strategy toward revolutionizing Iran. Secularism, democracy, and women's rights are thus today's leading themes in the group's strategic communications. As for Maryam Rajavi's leadership, in 2017 it appears to be political and cultural; any remnants of a military force and interest in terrorist strategies have faded away."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarmonBowdish2018166-319]

### Cult of personality

The MEK has been described as a [cult of personality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cult_of_personality) by a variety of sources.[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323] The MEK has been described as a "cult" by the Iranian government and Iraqi politician [Samir Sumaidaie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samir_Sumaidaie).[^cite_note-324] On May 25, 1981, Khomeini appeared on national television accusing those who criticised the [Islamic Consultative Assembly](383276.md)'s decisions of having a cult of personality.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian198966–67-325]

It has also been described as a cult by the United States government, and another retired United States general described it as "Cult? How about admirably focused group?".[^cite_note-326] [Romain Nadal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romain_Nadal) said the MEK had a "cult nature", and [Bernard Kouchner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Kouchner) said he was ashamed by Nadal's criticism.[^cite_note-327] Also numerous academics[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989260–261-328][^cite_note-329][^cite_note-Saeed_Kamali-66][^cite_note-330] and former MEK members who [defected](383663.md)[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332] have described it as a cult.[^cite_note-333]

Some sources argue that the Iranian government exploits such allegations to demonise the MEK.[^cite_note-334] The Iranian government is reportedly running a disinformation campaign to discredit the MEK, with the head of the Mackenzie Institute commenting that "Iran is trying to get other countries to label it as a terrorist cult".[^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336] According to a [RAND Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAND_Corporation) report for the US government, during Masoud Rajavi's "ideological revolution", members were required to give "near-religious devotion" to its leaders. Also according to RAND, the MEK had "many of the typical characteristics of a cult, such as authoritarian control, confiscation of assets, sexual control (including mandatory divorce and celibacy), emotional isolation, forced labour, sleep deprivation, physical abuse and limited exit options," while this is vehemently denied by its supporters and leaders.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson2009-337] [United Press International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Press_International) (UPI) said that "The truth is that the group's ideology has evolved over the years in order to adapt with the region's geopolitical changes."[^cite_note-338]

In 1990 MEK leadership ordered all couples to divorce, forbid them from remarrying, and children were sent away.[^cite_note-BBC1-339][^cite_note-merat2018-38] Children were removed from the MEK camp because MEK "resistance fighters" are required to dedicate themselves to their cause.[^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341] Critics often describe the MEK as the "cult of Rajavi", arguing that it revolves around the husband-and-wife duo, Maryam and Massoud Rajavi.[^cite_note-Rubin-217][^cite_note-auto12-342] Members reportedly had to participate in regular "ideological cleansings".[^cite_note-343] According to RAND, members were lured in through "false promises of employment, land, aid in applying for asylum in Western countries" and then prevented from leaving.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson2009-337] Masoud Banisadr, a vocal former member, suggested that the MEK had become a cult in order to survive.[^cite_note-r2016-344][^cite_note-345]

## Structure and organisation

### Organisations

Alongside its central organisation, the PMOI has a political wing, the [National Council of Resistance of Iran](1181356.md) (NCRI), established in 1981 with the stated goal of uniting the opposition to the Iranian government under one [umbrella organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umbrella_organization). The organisation has the appearance of a broad-based coalition, but analysts consider NCRI and MEK to be synonymous and recognise the NCRI as an only "nominally independent" political wing of the PMOI.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman200197-25][^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348] In 2002 the [FBI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI) reported that the NCRI has always been "an integral part" of the MEK and its "political branch".[^cite_note-auto13-349]

The PMOI also historically maintained a dedicated armed wing known as the National Liberation Army of Iran (NLA) that was established in 1987 to serve as an infantry force and coordinate the different militant groups members of the NCRI.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiazza199420-174] It was formally disbanded in 2003 during the [Iraq war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_war).[^cite_note-350]

Through its history, the MEK has maintained several front organisations including the Association of Iranian Scholars and Professionals, the Association of Iranian Women, Iran Aid, the California Society for Democracy, the Iranian-American Community of Northern Virginia and the Union Against Fundamentalism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarmonBowdish2018301-351][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClark201670-352]

### Membership

Before the Iran–Iraq War, the MEK was estimated to have about 2,000 members, peaking at 10,000 to 15,000 during the 1980s.[^cite_note-355] In the 2000s, the organisation had between 5,000 and 10,000 members, with 2,900 to 3,400 at [Camp Ashraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Ashraf).[^cite_note-membership-current-7] In February 2020, the MEK claimed [to have 2,500 members in its Albania camp](#Settlement_in_Albania_\(2016–present\)); a *[New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times)* reporter visiting the camp estimated 200 people were present over two days.[^cite_note-Iran_MEK_Albania-115]

### Fundraising

During its life in exile, MEK was initially financed by backers including [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein),[^cite_note-358][^cite_note-auto-359][^cite_note-360][^cite_note-auto11-361] and later a network of fake charities based in European countries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClark201673–74-362][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson200959-363][^cite_note-364]

In 2004, a report by the US weapons inspector Charles Duelfer claimed that Saddam Hussein provided millions of dollars from the [United Nations](31769.md)' [Oil-for-Food programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil-for-Food_program) to the MEK.[^cite_note-auto11-361][^cite_note-auto-359][^cite_note-365]

In [Germany](11867.md), the MEK used a NGO to "support asylum seekers and refugees". Another alleged organisation collected funds for "children whose parents had been killed in Iran" in sealed and stamped boxes placed in city centres. According to the Nejat Society, in 1988, the Nuremberg MEK front organisation was uncovered by [police](1826396.md). Initially, [The Greens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_'90/The_Greens) supported these organisations while it was unaware of their purpose.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClark201673–74-362]

In 1999, [United States](3434750.md) authorities arrested 29 individuals in Operation Eastern Approach,[^cite_note-CNN-207] of whom 15 were held on charges of helping MEK members illegally enter the US.[^cite_note-Rosenzweig-208] The ringleader pleaded guilty to providing phony documents to MEK members and violation of [Antiterrorism and Effective Death Penalty Act of 1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiterrorism_and_Effective_Death_Penalty_Act_of_1996).[^cite_note-articles.latimes.com-209][^cite_note-Californian_pleads_guilty_to_aiding-210]

The MEK also operated a [UK](31717.md)-based charity, Iran Aid, which claimed to raise money for Iranian refugees. In 2001, the [Charity Commission for England and Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charity_Commission_for_England_and_Wales) closed it down after finding no "verifiable links between the money donated by the British public \[approximately £5 million annually] and charitable work in Iran".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson200959-363][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClark201673-310][^cite_note-Tank-girl-366]

In December 2001, a joint FBI-[Cologne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cologne) police operation discovered what a 2004 report calls "a complex fraud scheme involving children and social benefits", involving the sister of [Maryam Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryam_Rajavi).[^cite_note-FBI2004-367] The [High Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Court) ruled to close several MEK compounds after investigations revealed that the organisation fraudulently collected between \$5 million and \$10 million in [social welfare benefits for children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_in_Germany) of its members sent to Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson200959-363]

In 2003, [General Intelligence and Security Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Intelligence_and_Security_Service) (AIVD) claimed that Netherlands charity that raises money for "children who suffer under the Iranian regime" (SIM ([Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language): *Stichting Solidariteit met Iraanse Mensen*)) was fundraising for the MEK. A spokesperson for the charity said that SIM was unrelated to the MEK and that these allegations were "lies from the Iranian regime".[^cite_note-auto1-190]

As [RAND Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAND_Corporation) policy reported, MEK supporters seek donations at public places, often showing "gruesome pictures" of [human rights victims in Iran](3156692.md) and claiming to raise money for them but funnelling it to MEK.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson200959-363] A 2004 report by [Federal Bureau of Investigation](11127.md) (FBI) states that the organisation is engaged "through a complex international money laundering operation that uses accounts in Turkey, Germany, France, Belgium, Norway, Sweden, Jordan, and the United Arab Emirates".[^cite_note-FBI2004-367]

On 19 November 2004, two front organisations called the Iranian–American Community of Northern Virginia and the Union Against Fundamentalism organised demonstrations in front of the [Capitol building](31979.md) in [Washington, D.C.](108956.md), and transferred funds for the demonstration, some \$9,000 to the account of a Texas MEK member. Congress and the bank in question were not aware that the demonstrators were actually providing material support to the MEK.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClark201673-310] According to [Spiegel Online](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiegel_Online) security experts say that U.S., Saudi Arabia and Israel provide the group with financial support, though there is no proof for this supposition and MEK denies this.[^cite_note-sp-online-215] The Hamburg state court ordered [Der Spiegel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Der_Spiegel) in 2019 to remove unsupported claims from an article that accused the MEK of "torture" and "psychoterror."[^cite_note-368]

### Intelligence capabilities

During the years MEK was based in Iraq, it was closely associated with the intelligence service *[Mukhabarat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Intelligence_Service)* (IIS),[^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370] and even had a dedicated department in the agency. [Directorate 14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directorate_14) of the IIS worked with the MEK in joint operations while Directorate 18 was exclusively responsible for the MEK and issued the orders and tasks for their operations.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372] The MEK offered IIS with intelligence it gathered from Iran, [interrogation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interrogation) and translation services.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson200961-373]

A 2008 report by the [United States Army Intelligence Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Army_Intelligence_Center), states that the MEK operates a [HUMINT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_intelligence_\(intelligence_gathering\)) network within Iran, which is "clearly a MEK core strength". It has started a debate among intelligence experts that "whether western powers should leverage this capability to better inform their own intelligence picture of the Iranian regime's goals and intentions".[^cite_note-374] [Rick Francona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rick_Francona) told *Foreign Policy* in 2005 that the MEK teams could work in conjunction with collection of intelligence and identifying agents. U.S. security officials maintain that the organisation has a record of exaggerating or fabricating information, according to *Newsweek*. [David Kay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Kay) believes that "they're often wrong, but occasionally they give you something".[^cite_note-375]

American government sources told *Newsweek* in 2005 that the [Pentagon](7279897.md) is hoping to utilise MEK members as [informants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informant) or give them training as [spies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spy) for use against Tehran.[^cite_note-376]

The MEK is able to conduct "telephone intelligence" operations effectively, i.e. gathering intelligence through making phone calls to officials and government organisations in Iran.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2009-377] According to [Ariane Tabatabai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariane_Tabatabai), the MEK's "capabilities to conduct terrorist attacks may have decreased in recent years."[^cite_note-378]

### Propaganda and social media

The MEK's first act of counter-propaganda was to release about 2014 Iranian prisoners of war within a period of 9 months. It started on 11 March 1986 when the NLA released 370 prisoners of war. They then released 170 prisoners of war in November 1987 that had been captured by the NLA. A third wave of 1300 prisoners of war were released in August 1988, with some joining the NLA ranks. During the last release, Massoud Rajavi promoted it this as an act of compassion by the NCRI, which was in contrast to the Islamic Republic's "cruel manner of treating" prisoners of war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2018-60] In the 1980s and the 1990s, their propaganda was mainly targeted against the officials in the establishment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarmonBowdish2018166-319] According to [Anthony H. Cordesman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_H._Cordesman), since the mid-1980s the MEK has confronted Iranian representatives overseas through "propaganda and street demonstrations".[^cite_note-379] Other analysts have also alleged that there is a propaganda campaign by the MEK in the West, including [Christopher C. Harmon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_C._Harmon)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarmonBowdish2018165–167-380] and Wilfried Buchta,[^cite_note-381] and others.[^cite_note-ap2014-382]

According to Kenneth Katzman, the MEK is able to mobilise its exile supporters in demonstration and fundraising campaigns. The organisation attempts to publicise regime abuses and curb foreign governments' relations with Tehran. To do so, it frequently conducts anti-regime marches and demonstrations in those countries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001105-69]

A 1986 U.S. State Department letter to KSCI-TV described "MEK propaganda" as being in line with the following: "\[T]he Iranian government is bad, the PMOI is against the Iranian government, the Iranian government represses the PMOI, therefore, the PMOI and its leader Rajavi are good and worth of support."[^cite_note-383] According to Masoud Kazemzadeh, the MEK has also used propaganda against defectors of the organisation.[^cite_note-384]

[Al Jazeera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Jazeera_Arabic) reported on an alleged Twitter-based MEK campaign. According to Exeter University lecturer Marc Owen Jones, accounts tweeting #FreeIran and #Iran_Regime_Change "were created within about a four-month window", suggesting bot activity.[^cite_note-385]

In an article published by *[The Intercept](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Intercept)* on 9 June 2019, two former MEK members claimed that "Heshmat Alavi" is not a real person, and that the articles published under that name were actually written by a team of people at the political wing of MEK. Alavi contributed to several media outlets including *[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)*, *[The Diplomat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Diplomat_\(magazine\))*, *[The Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hill_\(newspaper\))*, *[The Daily Caller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Daily_Caller)*, *[The Federalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Federalist_\(website\))* and the [English edition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Arabiya_English) of [Al Arabiya](321645.md)'s website. According to *The Intercept*, one of Alavi's articles published by *Forbes* was used by the [White House](33057.md) to justify [Donald Trump Administration](52231341.md)'s [sanctions against Iran](9941859.md).[^cite_note-TheIntercept-19JUN09-HeshmatAlaviNotReal-386] Since the article's publication, [Twitter](9988187.md) has suspended the "Heshmat Alavi" account, and the writings in the name of "Heshmat Alavi" were removed from *The Diplomat* and *Forbes'* website.[^cite_note-TheIntercept-19JUN09-HeshmatAlaviNotReal-386] A website purported to be a personal blog of "Heshmat Alavi" published a post with counterclaims saying that their Twitter account had been suspended.[^cite_note-TheIntercept-19JUN09-HeshmatAlaviNotReal-386][^cite_note-387]

## Terrorist designation

See also: [List of designated terrorist groups](63578.md)

### Assignment of designation

The countries and organisations below have officially listed MEK as a terrorist organisation:

| Currently listed by | [Iran](14653.md) | Designated by the current government[^cite_note-388] since 1981, also during [Pahlavi dynasty](338883.md)[^cite_note-389] until 1979 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Iraq](7515928.md) | Designated by the post-2003 government[^cite_note-cah-256][^cite_note-390] |  |
| Formerly listed by | [United States](3434750.md) | Designated on 8 July 1997, delisted on 28 September 2012[^cite_note-hoc-391] |
| [United Kingdom](31717.md) | Designated on 28 March 2001,[^cite_note-hoc-391] delisted on 24 June 2008[^cite_note-hoc-391] |  |
| [European Union](9317.md) | Designated in May 2002,[^cite_note-hoc-391] delisted on 26 January 2009[^cite_note-hoc-391] |  |
| [Japan](15573.md) | Designated on 5 July 2002,[^cite_note-392] delisted on 24 March 2013[^cite_note-JapanDesignation-393] |  |
| [Canada](5042916.md) | Designated on 24 May 2005,[^cite_note-394] delisted on 20 December 2012[^cite_note-canada-delist-395] |  |
| Other designations | [Australia](4689264.md) | Not designated as terrorist but added to the 'Consolidated List' subject to the [United Nations Security Council Resolution 1373](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1373) on 21 December 2001[^cite_note-396] Removed from the Consolidated List in late 2016.[^cite_note-397] |
| [United Nations](31769.md) | The group was described as "involved in terrorist activities" by the [United Nations Committee against Torture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Convention_against_Torture#Committee_against_Torture) in 2008[^cite_note-United_Nations_Committee_against_Torture_2008-398] |  |

In 1997, the United States put the MEK on the [U.S. State Department list of Foreign Terrorist Organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._State_Department_list_of_Foreign_Terrorist_Organizations).[^cite_note-bdt45cgf112-73][^cite_note-hersh2012-399] The *[Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times)* reported a senior official of the Clinton administration as saying that the designation of the MEK as a terrorist group "was intended as a goodwill gesture to Tehran and its newly elected president, Mohammad Khatami".[^cite_note-400][^cite_note-bdt45cgf112-73]

In 2004, the United States also considered the group as "noncombatants" and "protected persons" under the [Geneva Conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Conventions).[^cite_note-401] In 2002, the European Union, pressured by Washington, added MEK to its terrorist list.[^cite_note-402] In 2009, the U.S. Secretary of State [Condoleezza Rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Condoleezza_Rice) denied the MEK its request to be delisted.[^cite_note-403] In 2008, the [United Nations Committee against Torture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Convention_Against_Torture) said the MEK was involved in terrorist activities.[^cite_note-United_Nations_Committee_against_Torture_2008-398]

After the [US invasion of Iraq](201936.md), the MEK had a strong support base in the United States to be removed from its list of Foreign Terrorists Organizations, consequently turning it into a legitimate actor.[^cite_note-auto8-31][^cite_note-404]

### Removal of designation

The United Kingdom lifted the MEK's designation as a terrorist group in June 2008,[^cite_note-405] followed by the [Council of the European Union](5866.md) on 26 January 2009.[^cite_note-Runner-406][^cite_note-Reuters2009-407] It was also lifted in the United States following a decision by U.S. Secretary of State [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton)[^cite_note-NYT_2012-228] on 21 September 2012 and lastly in Canada on 20 December 2012.[^cite_note-canada-delist-395]

The Council of the European Union removed the group's terrorist designation following the [Court of Justice of the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Justice_of_the_European_Union)'s 2008 ruling, which criticised France for failing to reveal new supposed evidence that the MEK posed a terrorist threat.[^cite_note-Runner-406] The EU courts declared that the listing was unlawful because of "serious procedural failures" and lack of evidence connecting the MEK with terrorist activities.[^cite_note-408] Delisting allowed MEK to pursue tens of millions of dollars in frozen assets[^cite_note-Reuters2009-407] and lobby in Europe for more funds. It also removed the terrorist label from MEK members at Camp Ashraf in Iraq.[^cite_note-409]



[Rudy Giuliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudy_Giuliani), [Newt Gingrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newt_Gingrich), [James T. Conway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_T._Conway), [Bill Richardson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Richardson) and other American politicians at the MEK event in 2018

On 28 September 2012, the U.S. State Department formally removed MEK from its official list of terrorist organisations, beating a 1 October deadline in an MEK lawsuit.[^cite_note-NYT_2012-228][^cite_note-gpo.gov-410] Secretary of State Clinton said in a statement that the decision was made because the MEK had renounced violence and had cooperated in closing their Iraqi paramilitary base.[^cite_note-Rudenial-411] It was reported that MEK was removed from the U.S. list of terrorist organisations after intensive lobbying by a bipartisan group of lawmakers.[^cite_note-Iran_MEK_Albania-115] An official denied that lobbying by well-known figures influenced the decision.[^cite_note-Rudenial-411][^cite_note-412] Some former U.S. officials vehemently reject the new status and believe the MEK has not changed its ways.[^cite_note-THS-413] MEK leaders began a lobbying campaign to be removed from the list by promoting the group as a viable opposition to the clerical regime in Iran.[^cite_note-414][^cite_note-415][^cite_note-merat2018-38] During 2011, lobbying firms DLA Piper, Akin Gump Strauss Hauer & Feld and DiGenova & Toensing were paid almost \$1,5 million by Iranian American organisations to lobby for delisting the MEK in the US.[^cite_note-416]

The MEK advocated to remove itself from the list of [Foreign Terrorist Organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_State_Department_list_of_Foreign_Terrorist_Organizations), having paid high-profile officials upwards of \$50,000 give speeches calling for delisting.[^cite_note-adr-417][^cite_note-NYT-418] [Ervand Abrahamian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ervand_Abrahamian), [Shaul Bakhash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaul_Bakhash), [Juan Cole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Cole) and [Gary Sick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Sick) among others, published "Joint Experts' Statement on the Mujahedin-e Khalq" on *[Financial Times](136566.md)* voicing their concerns regarding MEK delisting.[^cite_note-419] The [National Iranian American Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Iranian_American_Council) denounced the decision, stating it "opens the door to Congressional funding of the M.E.K. to conduct terrorist attacks in Iran" and "makes war with Iran far more likely."[^cite_note-NYT_2012-228] Iran state television also condemned the delisting of the group, saying that the U.S. considers MEK to be "good terrorists because the U.S. is using them against Iran".[^cite_note-420]

The campaign to delist the MEK in the European Union counted with Spanish MEP [Alejo Vidal-Quadras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejo_Vidal-Quadras_Roca) as one of its lobbyists. [Vox](41928488.md), the far-right party he founded, later received funding by the [National Council of Resistance of Iran](1181356.md). The party received almost €1 million between December 2013 and April 2014.[^cite_note-vox-lobby-421]

## Foreign relations



Letter in Persian requesting that the Central Committee of the Communist Party of the Soviet Union lend any amount of money (up to US\$300,000,000) to the Mojahedin Organization and requesting that the supporters of the Mojahedin Organization be allowed to cross the Soviet-Iranian border and be granted a temporary asylum. Memorandum to the CK KPSS from Olfat.

[^cite_note-422]

While dealing with anti-regime clergy in 1974, the MEK became close with secular Left groups in and outside Iran. These included the confederation of Iranian Students, The [People's Democratic Republic of Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Democratic_Republic_of_Yemen) (South Yemen), and the [Popular Front for the Liberation of Oman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Front_for_the_Liberation_of_Oman), among others.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989152-154-423] The MEK sent five trained members into South Yemen to fight in the [Dhofar Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhofar_War) against Omani and Iranian forces.[^cite_note-424]

On 7 January 1986, the MEK leaders sent a twelve-page letter to the "comrades" of [Central Committee of the Communist Party of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Committee_of_the_Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union), asking for temporary asylum and a loan of \$300 million to continue their "revolutionary anti-imperialist" actions. It is not clear how the Soviets responded, according to [Abbas Milani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas_Milani).[^cite_note-auto14-425]

[Israel](9282173.md)'s foreign intelligence agency [Mossad](27619007.md) maintains connections with the MEK, dating back to the 1990s.[^cite_note-426] Until 2001, the MEK received support from the [Taliban](30635.md).[^cite_note-taliban-427] The MEK was also among the opposition groups receiving support from Gulf nations such as [Saudi Arabia](349303.md).[^cite_note-428]

In April 2012, journalist [Seymour Hersh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seymour_Hersh) reported that the U.S. [Joint Special Operations Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Special_Operations_Command) had trained MEK operatives at a secret site in [Nevada](21216.md) from 2005 to 2009. According to Hersh, MEK members were trained in intercepting communications, cryptography, weaponry and small unit tactics at the Nevada site up until President Barack Obama took office in 2009.[^cite_note-hersh2012-399]

### Position on the Israel–Palestinian conflict

See also: [Black September § Iranian guerillas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_September#Iranian_guerillas)

Initially, the MEK used to criticise the [Pahlavi dynasty](338883.md) for allying with [Israel](9282173.md) and [Apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid) [South Africa](17416221.md),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian198998-429] calling them racist states and demanding cancellation of all political and economic agreements with them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989185-430] The MEK opposed [Israeli–Palestinian peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli%E2%80%93Palestinian_peace_process)[^cite_note-431] and was [anti-Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Zionist).[^cite_note-Masoud_Banisadr-432]

The MEK's Central Cadre established contact with the [Palestine Liberation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Liberation_Organization) (PLO), by sending emissaries to Paris, Dubai, and Qatar to meet PLO officials.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989127-433] On 3 August 1972, they bombed the Jordanian embassy as a means to avenge [King Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein_of_Jordan)'s [unleashing his troops on the PLO in 1970](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_September).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989140-434]

### Relations with the United States

In the late 1970s, the intelligentsia as a class in Iran was distinctly nationalistic and anti-imperialistic. The MEK had impeccable nationalistic credentials, calling for the nationalisation of foreign companies and economic independence from the capitalist world, and praising writers such as [Al-e Ahmad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalal_Al-e-Ahmad), [Saedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gholam-Hossein_Sa'edi) and [Shariati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Shariati) for being "anti-imperialist".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989229-435] Rajavi in his presidential campaign after revolution used to warn against what he called the "imperialist danger."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989197-120] The matter was so fundamental to MEK that it criticised the Iranian government on that basis, accusing the Islamic Republic of "capitulation to imperialism" and being disloyal to democracy that according to [Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massoud_Rajavi) was the only means to "safeguard from American imperialism."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989209-436]

After exile, the MEK sought the support of prominent politicians, academics and human rights lawyers. Rajavi tried to reach as broad a Western public as possible by giving frequent interviews to Western newspapers. In these interviews, Rajavi toned down the issues of [imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialism), [foreign policy](233404.md), and [social revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_revolution). Instead, he stressed the themes of democracy, political liberties, political pluralism, human rights, respect for 'personal property,' the plight of political prisoners, and the need to end the senseless war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989245-437]

Hyeran Jo, associate professor of [Texas A\&M University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_A\&M_University) wrote in 2015 that the MEK is supported by the [United States](3434750.md).[^cite_note-438] In January 1993, President-elect Clinton wrote a private letter to Massoud Rajavi, in which he set out his support for the organisation.[^cite_note-439] The organisation has also received support United States officials including [Tom Ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Ridge), [Howard Dean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Dean), [Michael Mukasey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Mukasey), [Louis Freeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Freeh), [Hugh Shelton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugh_Shelton), [Rudy Giuliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudy_Giuliani), [John Bolton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_R._Bolton), [Bill Richardson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Richardson), [James L. Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_L._Jones), and [Edward G. Rendell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_G._Rendell).[^cite_note-440][^cite_note-441]

As Mukasey mentioned in *[The New York Times](30680.md)*, in 2011 he had received \$15,000 to \$20,000 to present a lecture about "MEK-related events", as well as what he listed as "a foreign agent lobbying pro bono for MEK's political arm".[^cite_note-NBC-Giuliani-442] Rendell said he had been paid to speak in support of the MEK[^cite_note-443] and Hamilton said he was paid to "appear on a panel Feb. 19 at the Mayflower Hotel in Washington."[^cite_note-444] In February 2015, *[The Intercept](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Intercept)* published that [Bob Menendez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Menendez), [John McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain), [Judy Chu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judy_Chu), [Dana Rohrabacher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dana_Rohrabacher) and [Robert Torricelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Torricelli) received campaign contributions from MEK supporters.[^cite_note-445]

Some politicians have declared receiving payment for supporting the MEK, but others support the group without payment.[^cite_note-446][^cite_note-Saeed_Kamali-66][^cite_note-447] In May 2018, [Daniel Benjamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Benjamin) who held office as the [Coordinator for Counterterrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coordinator_for_Counterterrorism) between 2009 and 2012, told *[The New York Times](30680.md)* that the MEK offered him money in exchange for his support.[^cite_note-448]

## Human rights record

In 2006, Iraqi Prime Minister [Al-Maliki](4723094.md) told the MEK it had to leave Iraq, but the MEK responded that the "request violated their status under the Geneva Convention". Al-Maliki and the [Iraqi Ministry of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_\(Iraq\)) maintained that the MEK had committed human rights abuses in the early 1990s when it aided Saddam Hussain's campaign against the Shia uprising.[^cite_note-449] According to *[Time magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))*, the MEK has denied aiding Saddam in quashing Kurdish and Shia rebellions.[^cite_note-450]

In May 2005, [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) (HRW) issued a report describing [prison](19008450.md) camps run by the MEK and severe [human rights violations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_violations) committed by the group against its members, ranging from prolonged incommunicado and [solitary confinement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solitary_confinement) to beatings, [verbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verbal_abuse) and [psychological abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_abuse), [coerced confessions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_confession), threats of [execution](5902.md), and [torture](47702.md) that in two cases led to death.[^cite_note-451] This report was disputed by the UK's [Lord Corbett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Corbett,_Baron_Corbett_of_Castle_Vale).[^cite_note-hoc-391][^cite_note-Tank-girl-366] Human Rights Watch released a statement in February 2006 stating the criticisms they received concerning the substance and methodology of the report were unwarranted.[^cite_note-452]

Former American military officers who had aided in guarding the MEK camp in Iraq gave differing accounts. Those suggested by MEK said its members had been free to leave the camp and that they had not found any prison or torture facilities. Captain Woodside who was not one of those who MEK suggested, said that US officers did not have regular access to camp buildings, or to group members and that it was difficult for members to leave.[^cite_note-Iran_MEK_Albania-115] Jo Hyeran, in her work examining humanitarian violations of rebel groups to international law, states that the MEK has not accepted [International Committee of the Red Cross](5023832.md) (ICRC) visits to its detention centres.[^cite_note-453] According to criticism of Human Right groups, marriage had been banned in the camp.[^cite_note-454] Upon entry into the group, new members are [indoctrinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indoctrinate) in ideology and a [revisionist history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_revisionism_\(negationism\)) of Iran. All members are required to participate in weekly "ideologic cleansings".[^cite_note-455] Members who [defected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defected) from the MEK and some experts say that these [Mao-style self-criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_and_self-criticism_\(Marxism%E2%80%93Leninism\)) sessions are intended to enforce control over sex and marriage in the organisation as a [total institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_institution).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarmonBowdish2018170-308] MEK denied the brainwashing describing it as part of Iranian 'misinformation campaign.'[^cite_note-Iran_MEK_Albania-115][^cite_note-456] Also [Abbas Milani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas_Milani) calls those describing MEK as a cult as lobbyists paid by Iranian regime.[^cite_note-auto14-425] In July 2020 a German court ordered the Frankfurter Allgemeine Zeitung to remove false information about the MEK.[^cite_note-457]

## Intelligence campaigns against the MEK

The Shah's regime waged a propaganda campaign against the MEK, accusing them "of carrying out subversive acts at the behest of their foreign patrons" and claiming that "the shoot-outs and bombings caused heavy casualties among bystanders and innocent civilians, especially women and children". It also obtained "public confessions" that accused former colleagues of crimes including sexual promiscuity. The regime claimed that the MEK were "unbelievers masquerading as Muslims", and used the Quranic term "monafeqin" (hypocrites) to describe them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian19892,_143–144,_256-458]

The [Islamic Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republican_Party) later used many of the same tactics, labelling the MEK "Marxist hypocrites and Western-contaminated 'electics', and as 'counter-revolutionary terrorists' collaborating with the Iraqi Ba'thists and the imperialists".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian19892,_143–144,_256-458] After the [1994 Imam Reza shrine bomb explosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Imam_Reza_shrine_bomb_explosion) in Mashhad which killed 25 and wounded at least 70 people, the Iranian regime immediately blamed the MEK. A month after the attack, a Sunni group calling itself "al-haraka al-islamiya al-iraniya" claimed responsibility for the attack. Despite this, the Iranian government continued to hold the MEK responsible for both attacks.[^cite_note-459] According to an anonymous U.S. official, [Ramzi Yousef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramzi_Yousef) built the bomb and MEK agents placed it in the shrine.[^cite_note-brianwilliams2012-460]

Even into the 2000s, the MEK has remained a major target of Iran's internal security apparatus.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001104-461] Since 2001, several reports by Dutch, German and US intelligence services have noted the ongoing efforts by the [Iran's Ministry of Intelligence](501536.md) to "track down and identify those who are in contact with opposition groups abroad", including the MEK.[^cite_note-auto4-462][^cite_note-Yonah-Alexander-463] German and US intelligence have noted that Iranian intelligence was directly financing a misinformation campaign and trying to recruit active or former members of opposition groups, sometimes through "threats to use force against them or their families living in Iran".[^cite_note-auto4-462][^cite_note-Security_2012,_p._26-464][^cite_note-465]

In 2018, U.S. District Court charged two alleged Iran agents of "conducting covert surveillance of Israeli and Jewish facilities in the United States and collecting intelligence on Americans linked to a political organization that wants to see the current Iranian government overthrown". During the court process, it was revealed that the two alleged agents of Iran had mostly gathered information concerning activities involving the MEK.[^cite_note-466] The two men pleaded guilty in November 2019 to several charges including conspiracy and "acting as an undeclared agent of the Iranian government". The Justice Department said that one of the men arrived in the US to gather "intelligence information" about the MEK (as well as Israeli and Jewish entities). The other admitted to taking photographs at a 2017 MEK rally in order to profile attendees.[^cite_note-467][^cite_note-468]

In January 2020, Ahmadreza Mohammadi-Doostdar, an Iranian-American, was sentenced by a U.S. court to 38 months in prison for conducting surveillance on American MEK members.[^cite_note-469] In September 2020 [The New York Times](30680.md) published a report where researchers alleged that opponents of the Iranian regime had been targets of a cyber attack by Iranian hackers through a variety of infiltration techniques. MEK was reportedly among the most prominent targets of the attacks.[^cite_note-470]

### Targeting of MEK members outside Iran

From 1989 to 1993, the Islamic Republic of Iran carried out numerous assassinations of MEK members. Between March and June 1990, three MEK members were assassinated in [Turkey](11125639.md). On 24 February 1990, Dr [Kazem Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazem_Rajavi) (a National Council member) was assassinated in [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva). In January 1993, an MEK member was murdered in Baghdad.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2018-60]

On 23 September 1991, an attempt was carried out to assassinate [Massoud Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massoud_Rajavi) in [Baghdad](4492.md). In August 1992, a MEK member was kidnapped and brought to Iran. In September 1992, MEK offices in Baghdad were broken into. In January 1993, a MEK bus was bombed without casualties. Towards the end of 1993, anonymous gunmen attacked [Air France](14998012.md) offices and the French embassy in Iran after France allowed [Maryam Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryam_Rajavi) and 200 MEK members to enter France.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2018-60]

In March 1993, the [NCRI](1181356.md)'s spokesman was murdered in [Italy](14532.md). In May 1990, a MEK member was murdered in [Cologne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cologne). In February 1993, a MEK member was murdered in [Manila](184334.md). In April 1992, a MEK member was murdered in the [Netherlands](21148.md). In August 1992, a MEK member was murdered in [Karachi](17123.md). In March 1993, two assassins on motorcycles murdered NCRI representative Mohammad Hossein Naqdi in [Italy](14532.md).[^cite_note-471] This led to the [European Parliament](9581.md) issuing a condemnation of the [Islamic Republic of Iran](14653.md) for political murder.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2018-60]

The Iranian regime is also believed to be responsible for killing NCR representative in 1993, and [Massoud Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massoud_Rajavi)'s brother in 1990. The MEK claims that in 1996 a shipment of Iranian mortars was intended for use by Iranian agents against [Maryam Rajavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryam_Rajavi).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001104-461] In May 1994, Islamic Republic agents assassinated two MEK members in [Iraq](7515928.md). In May 1995, five MEK members were assassinated in Iraq. In 1996, two MEK members were murdered in [Turkey](11125639.md) (including NCRI member Zahra Rajabi); in the same year two MEK members were killed in [Pakistan](23235.md) and another one in Iraq.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2018-60][^cite_note-472][^cite_note-473][^cite_note-474]

## Support

### Inside Iran

After the [1979 Iranian revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Iranian_revolution), the MEK gained significant support from the Iranian public, becoming the most popular dissident group.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoulkaHansellWilkeLarson20094,58-475][^cite_note-Iran_MEK_Albania-115] It also received support from national figures including intellectuals, military officers, and athletes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989243–246-476] Following the Iran–Iraq ceasefire, the Iranian regime executed Iranian citizens in western Iran accused of assisting the MEK. The regime continues to target MEK supporters in the present day.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen20181005–1006-477] Inside Iran, the strength of the MEK is uncertain since many of its supporters have been executed, tortured, or jailed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECimment201173-74-478][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2018-60] Its supporters within Iran have remained persistent, resisting the regime's attempts to eradicate the organisation from the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001104–105-163] Kenneth Katzman wrote in 2001 that the MEK is "Iran's most active opposition group".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman200197-25]

After becoming more violent and siding with [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein)'s Iraq during the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War) the MEK's standing inside Iran diminished.[^cite_note-popularity-28] Many Iranians maintain that the group cannot be forgiven for aligning with Iraq in the Iran–Iraq conflict.[^cite_note-479][^cite_note-popularity-28] [Karim Sadjadpour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karim_Sadjadpour) believes the MEK is a "fringe group with mysterious benefactors" with a negligible amount of supporters in Iran.[^cite_note-NBC-Giuliani-442] A 2009 report published by the [Brookings Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookings_Institution) notes that the organisation appears to be undemocratic and lacking popularity but maintains an operational presence in Iran, acting as a [proxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_war) against Tehran.[^cite_note-480] The group has been described as Iran's main political opposition group.[^cite_note-481][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiazza19949-482]

### Outside Iran

During the 1970s the group received assistance from the Liberation Movement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982491-302] In the 1980s, the MEK and the Kurdish Democratic Party, the National Democratic Front, the Hoviyat Group, and other groups joined the [National Council of Resistance of Iran](1181356.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1989243–246-476] Other groups opposing Khomeini's government, such as the [National Resistance Movement of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Resistance_Movement_of_Iran) (NAMIR), led by [Shapour Bakhtiar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shapour_Bakhtiar), criticised and rejected cooperation with the MEK.[^cite_note-Khonsari-483] The MEK has received political backing from American politicians advocating regime change in Iran.[^cite_note-484] Kenneth Katzman suggests that it's hard to determine the level of MEK support among Iran's exiles. While certain groups have distanced themselves from the organisation, others have lent their support.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatzman2001104–105-163]

## In the media

Main article: [List of works about the People's Mujahedin of Iran § Documentary films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_works_about_the_People's_Mujahedin_of_Iran#Documentary_films)

The MEK has been featured in several documentaries, including *A Cult That Would Be an Army: Cult of the Chameleon* (2007),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarmonBowdish2018300-485] *The Strange World of the People's Mujahedin* (2012)[^cite_note-486][^cite_note-487] and *[Midday Adventures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midday_Adventures)* (2017).[^cite_note-488]

"PMOI representatives contend that their organization has little alternative to its presence in Iraq if it is to have any chance of toppling the clerical regime."[Piazza 1994](#CITEREFPiazza1994), p. 10. "The deportation from Paris and move to Baghdad remains an intriguing and crucial episode in the history of the Mojahedin's exile. In examining both the accounts provided by the Islamic Republic's media sources and the press organs of the Mojahedin, it seems clear that the Khomeyni regime intended the Mojahedin to be exiled to an obscure and distant country which would weak their contacts with allied oppositions and keep them out of the European limelight. Instead, Iraq hastened to court the Mojahedin prior to its ousting, and the Islamic Republic found the opposition moved to a location which allowed the Mojahedin to resume its border raids"[Cohen 2009](#CITEREFCohen2009), p. 62-63. "Rajavi and a number of other Mojahedin members left their headquarters in Auvers-sur-Oise, a small town near Paris, on June 7, 1986 and boarded a plane to Baghdad. In the interim other European countries had refused to grant political asylum to the organization. Left with no other choice, and because they wanted to keep the organization intact, they therefore left for Iraq. The Mojahedin's official argument for relocating to Baghdad was that there they would be much closer geographically to their enemy, the Iranian Islamic Republic."[Keddie 2006](#CITEREFKeddie2006), p. 253: "In 1986 the French government forced them to leave Paris, and their center henceforth became Baghdad, Iraq, with which they were, until the U.S. 2003 victory in Iraq, allied."[Abrahamian 1989](#CITEREFAbrahamian1989), p. 197, 260. Finally, the Islamic Republic in June 1986 won another major victory in its campaign to isolate the Mojahedin. It persuaded the French government to close down the Mojahedin headquarters in Paris as a preliminary step towards improving Franco-Iranian relations... Unable to find refuge elsewhere in Europe, Rajavi put the best face possible on this defeat: he said that he was moving the Mojahedin headquarters to Iraq because they needed to be nearer to the armed struggle in Iran

[^cite_note-178]: &#x20;[Piazza 1994](#CITEREFPiazza1994): "On June 19, 1988, the NLA launched its offensive entitled Chehel Setareh or "40 Stars" in which twenty-two organized brigades of Mojahedin recaptured the city of Mehran, which the regime had wrested from Iraqi control after the Mojahedin had set up its "provisional government" there.
